NVIDIA TAO · How-To Reference Guide

How To Train, Fine-Tune, Evaluate, and Deploy NVIDIA TAO Models via the TAO Execution SDK

A single-page reference for working with the NVIDIA TAO Toolkit. Every workload in this guide is submitted through the TAO Execution SDK (tao_sdk) to a managed compute backend — NVIDIA Brev, SLURM, Kubernetes, or local Docker. Train and fine-tune computer vision, video understanding, vision-language, and 3D perception models; run AutoML hyperparameter optimization, data-efficient fine-tuning, Cosmos synthetic data generation, and k-NN data mining; export to ONNX and TensorRT; integrate with HuggingFace and Weights & Biases. The SDK abstracts the underlying scheduler, image pull, log collection, and S3 I/O — call sdk.create_job(...), get a job handle.

Topics covered: NVIDIA TAO Toolkit · TAO Execution SDK · BrevSDK · SlurmSDK · KubernetesSDK · DockerSDK · how to train a TAO model · how to fine-tune Hugging Face on TAO · AutoML · hyperparameter optimization (HPO) · bayesian search · hyperband · ASHA · BOHB · LLM-guided search · autoresearch · data-efficient fine-tuning loop · synthetic data generation (SDG) · Cosmos AnomalyGen · Cosmos Predict 2.5 · Cosmos Embed · Cosmos-Reason · k-NN mining · gap analysis · root cause analysis · Visual ChangeNet · ChangeNet · PCB defect detection · AOI · DINO · Deformable DETR · RT-DETR · Grounding DINO · Mask Grounding DINO · Mask2Former · OneFormer · SegFormer · CLIP · SigLIP2 · MAE · NVDINOv2 · NVPanoptix3D · BEVFusion · PointPillars · Sparse4D · OCDNet · OCRNet · CenterPose · Pose Classification · Action Recognition · Re-Identification · ML Recog · MAL · Optical Inspection · DepthNet Mono · DepthNet Stereo · FoundationStereo · VILA · video QA · VLM · object detection · semantic segmentation · instance segmentation · panoptic segmentation · image classification · embedding · retrieval · zero-shot · ONNX export · TensorRT · Brev · SLURM · Kubernetes · HuggingFace · HF_TOKEN · WandB · DAFT validate · DAFT convert · Qwen captioning · image grounding · referring expression · KITTI · video annotation · Chain-of-Thought.

About this guide. This is a standalone reference for the TAO Execution SDK (tao_sdk) — a new Python-based interface for submitting TAO workloads to managed compute backends. It is fully self-contained and independent of all previous TAO Toolkit releases. No prior experience with earlier TAO APIs, CLI tools, server infrastructure, or deployment patterns is assumed or required. All concepts and procedures used in this guide are defined here.

Getting Started

Three steps before you submit any workload: export the environment variables you need, install the TAO Execution SDK with the extra for your chosen platform, and pick the container image that the SDK will pull on your behalf. All training, evaluation, and inference is submitted through sdk.create_job(...) — you never invoke docker, sbatch, or kubectl directly, even when the chosen backend is your local Docker daemon (DockerSDK).

How To Use This Guide read first · humans & LLM agents

Human quick-start. This guide covers NVIDIA TAO Toolkit 7.x via the TAO Execution SDK. The SDK exposes one consistent call — sdk.create_job(image, command, gpu_count, …) — across four compute backends (Brev, SLURM, Kubernetes, Docker). The guide has three parts:

  • Getting Started — install the SDK, export credentials, choose a container image.
  • Models — one entry per network. Each entry has the container image tag, the action command, a full spec YAML template, and a ready-to-fill create_job snippet.
  • Platform — how to target Brev, SLURM, Kubernetes, or local Docker. Only the SDK class name and a few extra kwargs change; the job body is identical.

Typical path: read Getting Started → find your model in Models → find your platform in Platform → fill in <angle-bracket> placeholders with your values → run. If you are an LLM agent, keep reading — the rest of this section is your operating protocol.

Every code snippet in this guide is a template, not a runnable script. Anywhere you see angle brackets <like-this>, that value must come from the user — never from this guide. Collect each one before composing the final command.

Never pick an action input yourself — always ask. This applies to every input the action consumes: not just dataset/result URIs and spec values, but also gpu_count, num_nodes, the model / checkpoint (model_path), the compute backend, and any other <…> placeholder. The existence of a documented default does not authorize you to adopt it silently — present the input to the user in an explicit prompt before composing the command. When the guide names a default or a recommended value (e.g. a network's default model, or "one GPU fits this model"), suggest it in the prompt as the recommended option, but do not adopt it until the user confirms. If you find yourself about to write a concrete value the user never gave you, stop and ask first. Inferring a value from the hardware you can see (e.g. reading nvidia-smi to choose gpu_count) is still picking — surface what you found as a suggestion and let the user decide.

Convention. Throughout the guide, any value wrapped in <…> is a placeholder for the user's environment, dataset, or compute entitlements. Example call to the documented two-call SDK surface (build_entrypoint(...) + sdk.create_job(image, command, gpu_count, env_vars, **kwargs)):

from tao_sdk.script_runner import build_entrypoint

ep = build_entrypoint(
    command="<action-command>",                                       # <-- e.g. "dino train -e /specs/train.yaml"
    inputs={
        # Path-keyed: each URI is downloaded to the given container path
        # before the command runs. The spec YAML you edit references those
        # same container paths for its file-valued spec keys.
        "/specs/train.yaml":              "<edited-spec-uri>",         # <-- ASK the user
        "/data/train/images.tar.gz":      "<train-images-uri>",        # <-- ASK the user
        "/data/train/annotations.json":   "<train-annotations-uri>",
        "/data/eval/images.tar.gz":       "<eval-images-uri>",
        "/data/eval/annotations.json":    "<eval-annotations-uri>",
    },
    outputs=["/results/"],                                            # list of container paths to upload
)
job = sdk.create_job(
    image="<image>",                                                  # <-- chosen model entry's "Image" field
    command=ep["command"],
    gpu_count=<gpu-count>, num_nodes=<num-nodes>,                     # <-- ASK the user
    env_vars={"HF_TOKEN": os.environ.get("HF_TOKEN", "")},
    partition="<partition>", account="<account>",                     # <-- ASK the user (SlurmSDK-specific)
)
About inputs and outputs. inputs is a path-keyed dict: keys are container-side absolute paths and values are URIs (S3, HuggingFace, NGC, Lustre, or local). Each URI is downloaded to the matching container path before the command runs. outputs is a list of container paths to upload after the command finishes. The mapping from URIs to where the model finds its data is purely positional — the spec YAML you edit and the inputs dict must agree on the container paths.

The rule: if the user picks the value — even from a small library-defined set — wrap it in <…> and list the valid choices in the surrounding text. If the value is structural Python or a fixed name the library or the model dictates, keep it literal.

Stays literal: Python keyword arguments (image=, command=, gpu_count=), SDK class names, container image tags, spec key names (dataset.train_data_sources.image_dir — the keys of the nested specs dict), environment-variable names (NGC_KEY, HF_TOKEN, …), the SDK's standard container-side mount paths /data, /specs, /results, /ckpt — including when they appear in outputs=["/results/"] in passthrough mode (the path there must exactly match the path the user's command writes to, so the two co-vary as a literal pair — making one a placeholder would force the agent to coordinate two values that always have to be identical) — and per-model fixed values that the model entry documents (action command template, config_format).

Wrap in <…> (user-supplied): dataset / checkpoint / output URIs, numeric resource shapes (gpu_count, num_nodes, epochs, …), platform-specific kwargs values (partition, account, namespace, instance_id, …), and any value the user chooses from a library-defined set — including AutoML algorithm (<algorithm> ∈ {bayesian, hyperband, asha, bohb, dehb, pbt, llm, hybrid, autoresearch}), network architecture (<network> ∈ the model entries below), and action (<action> ∈ {train, evaluate, inference, export, gen_trt_engine, …, per model}).

Pre-flight protocol — follow this every time before taking any action (job submission, HTTP request to a running service, CLI command, or any other operation). Work through it top to bottom; ask the user about every unresolved item before writing or executing a single line of code:

  1. Identify required environment variables for the requested workflow (see How To Export All Required Environment Variables). For each variable that is required: ask the user "Is <VAR> already exported in your current shell?" and wait for confirmation. Do not assume any variable is set. Verify presence only — never read, print, or log secret values.
  2. Collect every unknown input before writing any code. Ask for anything not yet provided: compute backend (Brev / SLURM / Kubernetes / Docker), dataset or checkpoint URIs, required per-network spec keys (e.g. num_classes, monitoring metric), compute shape (gpu_count, num_nodes), platform-specific kwargs (partition, account, namespace, instance id), and — for inference requests — the actual prompt text and any media URLs. No placeholder value from this guide may remain unresolved when code executes.
  3. Confirm the container image — show the user the image you intend to use and wait for approval or an override.
  4. Install the TAO Execution SDK yourself for the chosen backend before executing — do not ask the user. Run pip install "nvidia-tao-sdk[<extra>]" (e.g. "nvidia-tao-sdk[docker]" for DockerSDK, "nvidia-tao-sdk[all]" to cover every backend). If it is already installed the command is a no-op.
  5. Add a timestamp to any workspace_path.

Hard rules:

  • Never execute any action — job submission, HTTP request, CLI command, or otherwise — using literal placeholder values from this guide.
  • Never guess dataset paths, class counts, bucket names, node-group names, GPU shapes, prompts, or media URLs.
  • Never claim to validate the user's local GPU or Docker setup — the SDK runs jobs on the chosen backend, not on the agent host.
  • Never write the user's secrets to a file. See How To Export All Required Environment Variables.
  • Always ask — never fall back to a script. If any item in the pre-flight list above is unresolved, ask the user about it directly. Resolve every unknown through conversation, then execute the action yourself. Do not write a script for the user to run. The only exception is when the user explicitly asks for a script.
  • After the pre-flight is complete and all inputs are confirmed, run the action immediately without asking for further permission — the user's request is the authorization.

When To Choose TAO positioning

TAO is purpose-built for teams that need NVIDIA-optimized computer vision, video, and 3D perception models trained on NVIDIA-managed compute with a direct path to TensorRT deployment. The guide below helps you decide whether TAO is the right fit for your project.

positioningwhy TAOcomparison

TAO differentiators

  • NGC pretrained model zoo. 40+ NVIDIA-optimized architectures (DINO, RT-DETR, BEVFusion, FoundationStereo, VILA, Cosmos, and more) with pretrained checkpoints hosted on NGC — no manual weight scraping or external storage needed for base weights.
  • Native NVIDIA hardware + TensorRT path. Every model exposes an export action that produces ONNX and a gen_trt_engine action that compiles a TensorRT engine — no manual ONNX surgery or separate trtexec scripts.
  • Multi-backend job dispatch. A single Python call (sdk.create_job(…)) runs on NVIDIA Brev, an on-prem SLURM cluster, Kubernetes, or the local Docker daemon — the SDK handles image pull, log collection, and S3 I/O for all of them.
  • AutoML with LLM guidance. Built-in hyperparameter optimization (Bayesian, Hyperband, ASHA, BOHB, DEHB, PBT) including an LLM-guided search mode that uses a language model as the HPO oracle.
  • Data-Efficient Fine-Tuning (DEFT). Iterative loops that chain k-NN mining, gap analysis, synthetic data generation (Cosmos), and retraining — reducing labeled-data requirements without changing model training code.
  • Cosmos synthetic data. Direct integration with Cosmos Predict 2.5, Cosmos AnomalyGen, and Cosmos-RL for video-based synthetic data generation — reducing annotation cost for hard-negative cases.
How TAO compares to common alternatives — click to expand
If you are evaluating… TAO is the better fit when… The alternative wins when…
HuggingFace AutoTrain / Transformers Your task is CV, video, or 3D perception rather than NLP; you need TensorRT deployment or NVIDIA-managed compute; you want DEFT or AutoML HPO without custom glue code. Your primary workload is text or multimodal NLP, your team is already deep in the HuggingFace ecosystem, or you need the widest possible model selection including non-NVIDIA-optimized checkpoints.
Ultralytics (YOLO) You need tasks beyond 2D object detection and segmentation: 3D perception (BEVFusion, Sparse4D, PointPillars), depth estimation, text detection/recognition, re-identification, pose estimation, or vision-language models. Your sole task is 2D detection or segmentation, fast iteration matters more than deployment optimization, and you do not need multi-node SLURM or Kubernetes job management.
Roboflow You already have labeled datasets and need training, AutoML, and deployment — TAO covers the full train-to-TRT pipeline on NVIDIA hardware. You need data annotation, labeling workflows, or dataset versioning — TAO is a training and deployment platform, not a data-management or labeling tool.
Vertex AI AutoML / Google Cloud Vision You are running on NVIDIA-backed infrastructure (DGX Cloud, on-prem SLURM, bare-metal Kubernetes) or you need model transparency, custom architecture selection, and TensorRT export. You are committed to GCP infrastructure and need a fully managed no-code service without Python SDK integration overhead.

How To Export All Required Environment Variables do this first

Security: Treat every value marked secret below as sensitive. Export them only as shell environment variables in the current session. Do not write secrets to any file — not .env, not a config file, not your shell rc file, not a notebook cell. Files leak (backups, sync clients, version control, screen-shares, log scrapers). When the shell exits, the value is gone.

Before launching your agent or running any command in this guide, export the variables relevant to the workflows you plan to run. Each later section assumes the relevant variables are already set in the shell that will spawn the workload. Skip rows for services you are not using.

Environment variables reference — click to expand
Variable Required? Used by What it is
NGC_KEY secret required Image pulls from nvcr.io on every platform NVIDIA NGC API key from ngc.nvidia.com. The SDK injects it into the backend's image-pull credentials
HF_TOKEN secret required for gated models Cosmos-RL, Cosmos-Embed, Visual ChangeNet, VILA, HuggingFace fine-tune HuggingFace user access token (read scope; write scope for HF Hub push)
BREV_API_TOKEN secret required for Brev NVIDIA Brev (BrevSDK) API token from the Brev console settings page
SLURM_USER required for SLURM SLURM (SlurmSDK) Your cluster login username
SLURM_HOSTNAME required for SLURM SLURM (SlurmSDK) Login host(s). Comma-separated list permitted for failover
SSH_KEY_PATH optional SLURM (SlurmSDK) Path to your private SSH key (defaults to ~/.ssh/id_ed25519). The key file itself must stay on disk; only its path is the env var
ACCESS_KEY secret required for S3 Any workflow with s3:// dataset or result URIs S3-compatible access key id
SECRET_KEY secret required for S3 Any workflow with s3:// dataset or result URIs S3-compatible secret access key
S3_ENDPOINT_URL optional S3-compatible storage Endpoint URL for non-AWS S3 (MinIO, Cloudflare R2, …). Omit for AWS S3
S3_BUCKET_NAME optional S3-compatible storage Bucket used as the default output destination
CLOUD_REGION optional S3-compatible storage AWS-style region string (e.g. us-east-1)
WANDB_API_KEY secret required for WandB AutoML tracking, HuggingFace fine-tune Weights & Biases API key from wandb.ai/authorize
WANDB_PROJECT optional AutoML tracking, HuggingFace fine-tune Project name to log runs under
AUTOML_LLM_ENDPOINT required for LLM AutoML AutoML algorithms llm, hybrid, autoresearch LLM endpoint URL (e.g. https://inference-api.nvidia.com)
AUTOML_LLM_MODEL required for LLM AutoML AutoML algorithms llm, hybrid, autoresearch Model identifier served at the endpoint (e.g. gcp/google/gemini-3.1-pro-preview)
AUTOML_LLM_API_KEY secret required for LLM AutoML AutoML algorithms llm, hybrid, autoresearch API key for the LLM endpoint. Takes precedence over NVIDIA_API_KEY
NVIDIA_API_KEY secret optional AutoML algorithms llm, hybrid, autoresearch NVIDIA Inference API key (nvapi-…). Fallback when AUTOML_LLM_API_KEY is not set
GOOGLE_API_KEY secret optional image-grounding, referring-expression, video-reasoning-annotation (Gemini backend) Google API key for Gemini VLM. Used when vlm.backend: "gemini" is set in the spec

Workflow: child processes only inherit variables that exist at the moment they are spawned, so an agent you started before exporting these will not see them. To pick them up:

  1. Exit your agent first. If it's running in this terminal (e.g. claude), quit it with /exit or Ctrl-D so the shell prompt comes back.
  2. Run the export commands below in that same shell. Replace each "…" placeholder with your actual value:
# NVIDIA NGC
export NGC_KEY="<ngc-api-key>"

# HuggingFace
export HF_TOKEN="<hf-token>"

# NVIDIA Brev
export BREV_API_TOKEN="<brev-api-token>"

# SLURM remote cluster
export SLURM_USER="<cluster-username>"
export SLURM_HOSTNAME="<login-host>[,<failover-login-host>]"
export SSH_KEY_PATH="$HOME/.ssh/id_ed25519"

# S3-compatible object storage
export ACCESS_KEY="<s3-access-key>"
export SECRET_KEY="<s3-secret-key>"
export S3_ENDPOINT_URL="<s3-endpoint-url>"
export S3_BUCKET_NAME="<bucket>"
export CLOUD_REGION="<region>"

# Weights & Biases
export WANDB_API_KEY="<wandb-api-key>"
export WANDB_PROJECT="<wandb-project>"

# LLM-guided AutoML
export AUTOML_LLM_ENDPOINT="<llm-endpoint-url>"
export AUTOML_LLM_MODEL="<llm-model-id>"
export AUTOML_LLM_API_KEY="<llm-api-key>"
export NVIDIA_API_KEY="<nvidia-api-key>"        # fallback when AUTOML_LLM_API_KEY is not set

# VLM-backed data pipelines (image grounding, referring expressions, video CoT)
export GOOGLE_API_KEY="<google-api-key>"        # Gemini backend

Then restart your agent from the same shell so it inherits the new variables. For Claude Code, resume the previous conversation in place:

claude --resume
Rule of thumb: child processes inherit only variables exported before they were spawned, and new shells do not inherit exports from other shells. So the sequence is always exit agent → export → restart agent. If you open a fresh terminal later, re-run the export commands there too before starting another agent.

How To Install the TAO Execution SDK required

The TAO Execution SDK (tao_sdk) is the Python package every workload in this guide uses. It exposes one consistent interface — create_job, get_job_status, get_job_logs, cancel_job — across all supported backends. The SDK handles image pulls, S3 input/output wrapping, log collection, multi-node coordination, and failure analysis on your behalf.

Install the SDK with the [all] extra — it pulls in every platform-specific dependency (Brev, SLURM, Kubernetes, Docker, S3 utilities) in a single command:

pip install "nvidia-tao-sdk[all]"
About the package index. The TAO SDK is published to an environment-specific Python index — the runner or container you install from is expected to have a working pip configuration (e.g. ~/.pip/pip.conf, PIP_INDEX_URL, PIP_EXTRA_INDEX_URL, or a proxy) that can resolve nvidia-tao-sdk. If the install fails with an index/network error, that's a runner-setup issue: confirm your pip can reach the configured index before re-trying. This guide stays agnostic to the registry.

Finer-grained extras exist if you only want one backend's dependencies — nvidia-tao-sdk[brev], [slurm], [kubernetes], [docker] — but [all] is the recommended path and the one the rest of this guide assumes.

Verify the install:

python3 -c "import tao_sdk; print(tao_sdk.__version__)"

How To Choose the Right TAO Container Image images

Every sdk.create_job(...) call takes an image kwarg. The SDK pulls the image on the backend on your behalf — you never run docker pull yourself. Use one of:

# tao-toolkit base images
nvcr.io/nvidia/tao/tao-toolkit:7.0.0-pyt                # PyTorch trainer (DINO, CLIP, SegFormer, ML-Recog, video-reasoning-annotation, …)
nvcr.io/nvidia/tao/tao-toolkit:7.0.0-deploy             # TAO Deploy (ONNX/TensorRT)
nvcr.io/nvidia/tao/tao-toolkit:7.0.0-data-services      # data services (image-grounding, referring-expressions, AOI gap analysis/mining)
nvcr.io/nvidia/tao/tao-toolkit:7.0.0-cosmos-rl          # Cosmos-Reason2-8B SFT
nvcr.io/nvidia/tao/tao-toolkit:7.0.0-cosmos-predict     # Cosmos Predict 2.5 (text-to-video SDG)
nvcr.io/nvidia/tao/tao-toolkit:7.0.0-cosmos-embed       # Cosmos-Embed1 video-text
nvcr.io/nvidia/tao/tao-toolkit:6.26.3-vila               # VILA VLM
nvcr.io/nvidia/tao/tao-toolkit:5.5.0-pyt                # BEVFusion only (requires mmdet3d)

# Specialty images
nvcr.io/nv-metropolis-dev/metropolis-sdg/cosmos-anomalygen:1.0.3-006434bb.main   # Cosmos AnomalyGen
nvcr.io/nvidian/iva/tao-toolkit-ds:aoi                                          # VCN AOI data services
nvcr.io/nvidian/iva/its-cosmos-sda                                              # Qwen captioning

Each model how-to in the next sections names the exact image to pass to the SDK.

How To Build the Spec YAML for a Training Run read before you submit a job

Every TAO action that reads a config file expects a complete spec — 100–500+ lines of nested YAML covering backbone, optimizer, augmentation, scheduler, loss weights, and dozens of other knobs. The full default spec for every model is inlined in this document — each per-model entry contains a collapsed "Full default spec template" block with the entire YAML. You do not need to fetch anything from outside this page.

The workflow is edit → upload → reference:

  1. Copy the model's full default spec YAML from its per-model entry under Models (the collapsed "Full default spec template" details block).
  2. Edit the values you need to override — dataset URIs, num_classes, num_epochs, num_gpus, etc. Leave every other key at the template default unless you have a specific reason to change it. For file-valued keys (e.g. dataset.train_data_sources[0].image_dir), set the value to a container-side path you pick — those paths must agree with the keys you pass in inputs={...} to build_entrypoint(...).
  3. Save the edited YAML somewhere reachable by a URI — upload it to S3, place it on Lustre, or keep it on the local filesystem if you're submitting through the local-Docker backend. Note its URI.
  4. Reference it from build_entrypoint(inputs={...}) when you build the entrypoint. The runtime downloads the URI to the container path you choose for the spec, plus every dataset URI to the paths the spec references.

Concrete pattern — the spec YAML is a text file the user prepares and uploads; build_entrypoint wraps the action command with the download + upload runtime, and sdk.create_job submits it. The spec is referenced inside the container at /specs/train.yaml:

import os
from tao_sdk.platforms.slurm import SlurmSDK
from tao_sdk.script_runner   import build_entrypoint

sdk = SlurmSDK()
ep  = build_entrypoint(
    command="<action-command>",                     # e.g. "dino train -e /specs/train.yaml"
    inputs={
        "/specs/train.yaml":                  "<edited-spec-uri>",
        # plus one entry per file the spec references — the container path on the
        # left MUST match the path you put inside the spec for that file-valued key.
        "/data/train/images.tar.gz":          "<train-images-uri>",
        "/data/train/annotations.json":       "<train-annotations-uri>",
        "/data/eval/images.tar.gz":           "<eval-images-uri>",
        "/data/eval/annotations.json":        "<eval-annotations-uri>",
    },
    outputs=["/results/"],
)
job = sdk.create_job(
    image="<image>",                                # from the chosen model entry's "Image" field
    command=ep["command"],
    gpu_count=<gpu-count>, num_nodes=<num-nodes>,
    env_vars={"HF_TOKEN": os.environ.get("HF_TOKEN", "")},
    partition="<partition>", account="<account>",    # platform-specific kwargs (SlurmSDK)
)
What if the template block is empty / missing for a model? A handful of models do not ship a default training-spec template in their entry. In that case the model's own entry documents the "Critical Overrides" — the minimal set of keys you must construct from scratch. Write a fresh YAML containing exactly those keys; do not invent unrelated keys, and do not paste defaults from a different model.

Applications · End-to-End Workflows

Applications compose models, data preparation, and a managed compute backend into multi-stage pipelines. Reach for them when you want hyperparameter sweeps, iterative data augmentation, cross-model RCA, or a HuggingFace-to-TAO bridge — instead of one-shot training. Every application submits its underlying jobs through the TAO Execution SDK.

Reminder: every code sample below contains placeholders (dataset URIs, image tags, num_classes, gpu_count, node group, workspace path, LLM endpoint). Resolve each one with the user before composing the final call — see How To Use This Guide.

How To Train Any TAO Model (single step) workflow

Standard supervised fine-tuning: train a model on a labeled dataset, optionally evaluate, then optionally export. The most common TAO workflow for adapting a pretrained model to a new dataset.

trainingsingle-stepgeneric
Step 0 — Confirm environment variables (before anything else in this flow — even after collecting the action inputs). Collect every action input from the user — never pick one yourself. This covers the model / checkpoint (model_path), gpu_count, num_nodes, the compute backend, and every other <…> placeholder: prompt the user for each before composing the command, and where the guide names a default or recommended value (or you could infer one from the hardware, e.g. reading nvidia-smi to size gpu_count), offer it as a suggestion in that prompt but do not adopt it until the user confirms. This action uses the variables listed below: required ones must already be exported in the shell that launched your agent; optional ones are only needed for the cases noted. Before executing any later step, ask the user to confirm that every required variable below has been set — expect a plain "yes" or "no, let me set them and restart." Ask the user directly; do not probe your own shell to decide this, since the agent's tool shell may not mirror the shell that launched the agent and can report a false "unset" for variables the user has in fact exported. Defer any actual environment check to the moment just before you run the code that consumes these variables. If any required variable is missing, stop here — have the user export it in the launching shell and restart the agent before continuing; do not set it mid-session, since later steps read it from the launching environment.
  • NGC_KEY — NGC API key (container image pulls from nvcr.io; get it at NGC →)
  • ACCESS_KEY + SECRET_KEY — S3-compatible credentials (only if dataset / results URIs use s3://)
  • HF_TOKEN — optional; only if the model checkpoint is gated on HuggingFace
  • WANDB_API_KEY — optional; only if wandb.enable: true in your spec
Also export credentials for your target compute backend — see the matching Platform how-to.

Pattern: pick a network and an SDK; call sdk.create_job once for train, again for evaluate, again for export. The SDK preserves results in your S3 bucket and gives you a job handle for status, logs, and cancellation.

Shared setup

Step 1 — Imports + image + reusable env.

import os
from tao_sdk.platforms.slurm import SlurmSDK   # or BrevSDK, KubernetesSDK, DockerSDK
from tao_sdk.script_runner   import build_entrypoint

sdk   = SlurmSDK()
IMAGE = "<image>"                                   # from the chosen model entry's "Image" field
ENV   = {"HF_TOKEN": os.environ.get("HF_TOKEN", "")}

Training the Model

Step 1 — Edit the model's training spec YAML. Copy the "Full default spec template" from the chosen model's entry under Models, fill in your overrides (dataset URIs, num_classes, num_epochs, num_gpus), and upload it to a URI you can reference below.

Step 2 — Submit the training job. Mount the spec at /specs/train.yaml and add one inputs entry per file-valued spec key the model consumes (see each model entry for the exact set).

ep_train = build_entrypoint(
    command="<network> train -e /specs/train.yaml",   # e.g. "dino train -e /specs/train.yaml"
    inputs={
        "/specs/train.yaml":              "<train-spec-uri>",
        # plus one entry per file the spec references — see each model entry
        "/data/train/images.tar.gz":      "<train-images-uri>",
        "/data/train/annotations.json":   "<train-annotations-uri>",
        "/data/eval/images.tar.gz":       "<eval-images-uri>",
        "/data/eval/annotations.json":    "<eval-annotations-uri>",
    },
    outputs=["/results/"],
)
train = sdk.create_job(
    image=IMAGE,
    command=ep_train["command"],
    gpu_count=<gpu-count>, num_nodes=<num-nodes>, env_vars=ENV,
    partition="<partition>", account="<account>",
)

Step 3 — Poll status.

print(sdk.get_job_status(train.id))

Evaluating the Trained Model

Step 1 — Edit the evaluate-action spec YAML. Point it at the trained checkpoint and the test dataset (see the model entry for which spec keys those are). Upload it.

Step 2 — Submit the evaluate job.

ep_eval = build_entrypoint(
    command="<network> evaluate -e /specs/evaluate.yaml",
    inputs={
        "/specs/evaluate.yaml":           "<evaluate-spec-uri>",
        "/data/eval/checkpoint.pth":      "<trained-checkpoint-uri>",
        "/data/eval/images.tar.gz":       "<eval-images-uri>",
        "/data/eval/annotations.json":    "<eval-annotations-uri>",
    },
    outputs=["/results/"],
)
evaluate = sdk.create_job(
    image=IMAGE,
    command=ep_eval["command"],
    gpu_count=<gpu-count>, env_vars=ENV,
)

Exporting the Model (ONNX)

Step 1 — Edit the export-action spec YAML.Point it at the trained checkpoint and upload it to a URI you can reference below.

Step 2 — Submit the export job.

ep_export = build_entrypoint(
    command="<network> export -e /specs/export.yaml",
    inputs={
        "/specs/export.yaml":          "<export-spec-uri>",
        "/data/export/checkpoint.pth": "<trained-checkpoint-uri>",
    },
    outputs=["/results/"],
)
export_ = sdk.create_job(
    image=IMAGE,
    command=ep_export["command"],
    gpu_count=<gpu-count>, env_vars=ENV,
)
Worked example — classification-pyt on local Docker (fully filled in) — click to expand

This example trains a ResNet-50 image classifier on a custom dataset, evaluates it, and exports to ONNX. All <angle-bracket> values are filled with concrete values so you can see exactly what a real job looks like.

import os
from tao_sdk.platforms.docker import DockerSDK
from tao_sdk.script_runner    import build_entrypoint

sdk   = DockerSDK()
IMAGE = "nvcr.io/nvidia/tao/tao-toolkit:7.0.0-pyt"
ENV   = {
    "NGC_KEY": os.environ["NGC_KEY"],   # image pull credential
}

# --- Step 1: Train ---
# Spec was copied from the "classification_pyt" model entry, edited with:
#   dataset.train_dataset: /data/train
#   dataset.val_dataset:   /data/val
#   model.num_classes:     10
#   train.num_epochs:      20
#   results_dir:           /results
ep_train = build_entrypoint(
    command="classification_pyt train -e /specs/train.yaml",
    inputs={
        "/specs/train.yaml":    "s3://my-bucket/specs/cls_train.yaml",
        "/data/train":          "s3://my-bucket/datasets/flowers/train",
        "/data/val":            "s3://my-bucket/datasets/flowers/val",
    },
    outputs=["/results/"],
)
train = sdk.create_job(image=IMAGE, command=ep_train["command"], gpu_count=1, env_vars=ENV)
print("train job:", train.id, sdk.get_job_status(train.id))

# --- Step 2: Evaluate ---
ep_eval = build_entrypoint(
    command="classification_pyt evaluate -e /specs/evaluate.yaml",
    inputs={
        "/specs/evaluate.yaml":         "s3://my-bucket/specs/cls_evaluate.yaml",
        "/data/checkpoint.pth":         "s3://my-bucket/results/train-run-001/model_best.pth",
        "/data/val":                    "s3://my-bucket/datasets/flowers/val",
    },
    outputs=["/results/"],
)
evaluate = sdk.create_job(image=IMAGE, command=ep_eval["command"], gpu_count=1, env_vars=ENV)
# Success metric: "top1_accuracy" printed to job logs — target ≥ 0.85 for flowers-10

# --- Step 3: Export to ONNX ---
ep_export = build_entrypoint(
    command="classification_pyt export -e /specs/export.yaml",
    inputs={
        "/specs/export.yaml":           "s3://my-bucket/specs/cls_export.yaml",
        "/data/checkpoint.pth":         "s3://my-bucket/results/train-run-001/model_best.pth",
    },
    outputs=["/results/"],
)
export_ = sdk.create_job(image=IMAGE, command=ep_export["command"], gpu_count=1, env_vars=ENV)
# Output artifact: /results/cls_resnet50.onnx — ready for TAO Deploy / TensorRT

To use a different backend, replace DockerSDK() with BrevSDK(), SlurmSDK(), etc., and add the backend-specific kwargs shown in the Platform section. The rest of the snippet is identical.

How To Run AutoML / Hyperparameter Optimization (HPO) workflow

Run automated hyperparameter optimization for any TAO network via AutoMLRunner. The runner wraps any TAO Execution SDK so AutoML is platform-agnostic — Brev, SLURM, Kubernetes, or local Docker. Algorithms: bayesian, hyperband, ASHA, BOHB, DEHB, PBT, LLM, hybrid, autoresearch.

automlhpobayesianhyperbandashallm-guidedautoresearchwandb
Step 0 — Confirm environment variables (before anything else in this flow — even after collecting the action inputs). Collect every action input from the user — never pick one yourself. This covers the model / checkpoint (model_path), gpu_count, num_nodes, the compute backend, and every other <…> placeholder: prompt the user for each before composing the command, and where the guide names a default or recommended value (or you could infer one from the hardware, e.g. reading nvidia-smi to size gpu_count), offer it as a suggestion in that prompt but do not adopt it until the user confirms. This action uses the variables listed below: required ones must already be exported in the shell that launched your agent; optional ones are only needed for the cases noted. Before executing any later step, ask the user to confirm that every required variable below has been set — expect a plain "yes" or "no, let me set them and restart." Ask the user directly; do not probe your own shell to decide this, since the agent's tool shell may not mirror the shell that launched the agent and can report a false "unset" for variables the user has in fact exported. Defer any actual environment check to the moment just before you run the code that consumes these variables. If any required variable is missing, stop here — have the user export it in the launching shell and restart the agent before continuing; do not set it mid-session, since later steps read it from the launching environment.
  • NGC_KEY — NGC API key (container image pulls from nvcr.io; get it at NGC →)
  • ACCESS_KEY + SECRET_KEY — S3-compatible credentials (only if workspace / dataset URIs use s3://)
  • AUTOML_LLM_ENDPOINT + AUTOML_LLM_MODEL + AUTOML_LLM_API_KEY — only if using the llm, hybrid, or autoresearch algorithm; falls back to NVIDIA_API_KEY if AUTOML_LLM_API_KEY is unset
  • WANDB_API_KEY — optional; only if WandB logging is enabled in the underlying model spec
Also export credentials for your target compute backend — see the matching Platform how-to.

Installing AutoML

Step 1 — Pick the platform extra and install. Replace <platform> with one of brev, slurm, kubernetes, docker, or all. Add the [llm] extra only if you plan to use the LLM-guided algorithms below.

REPO='git+https://gitlab-master.nvidia.com/nvidia-tao-toolkit/tao-run-automl.git'
pip install "nvidia-tao-automl[<platform>] @ $REPO"
pip install "nvidia-tao-automl[<platform>,llm] @ $REPO"   # only for LLM-guided algorithms

Step 2 — Verify the import.

python3 -c "from tao_automl.runner import AutoMLRunner; print('OK')"

Running a Classical Sweep (bayesian, hyperband, asha, …)

Valid values for <algorithm>: bayesian, hyperband, asha, bohb, dehb, pbt. Valid values for <action>: train is the typical choice; AutoML can also wrap other actions the model declares.

Step 1 — Imports + a fresh timestamped workspace.

import os
from datetime import datetime
from tao_sdk.platforms.slurm import SlurmSDK
from tao_automl.runner      import AutoMLRunner

TIMESTAMP = datetime.now().strftime("%Y%m%d_%H%M%S")

Step 2 — Construct the runner for the chosen network + action.

sdk    = SlurmSDK()
runner = AutoMLRunner(sdk=sdk, network_arch="<network>", action="<action>")

Step 3 — Run the sweep.

result = runner.run(
    train_dataset_uri="<train-dataset-uri>",
    automl_settings={
        "algorithm": "<algorithm>",
        "metric":    "<monitoring-metric>",              # e.g. mAP50 for DINO, val_mAP50 for Deformable DETR / Grounding DINO
        "automl_max_recommendations": <max-recs>,        # typical: 10
    },
    workspace_path=f"<workspace-uri>/{TIMESTAMP}",      # ALWAYS timestamp to avoid collisions
    gpu_count=<gpu-count>, num_nodes=<num-nodes>, partition="<partition>", account="<account>",
)
print("Best:", result["best"])

Running an LLM-Guided Sweep (llm, hybrid, autoresearch)

Same flow as the classical sweep, but the automl_settings dict adds the LLM endpoint fields. The library's hardcoded default endpoint returns 404 — always set llm_endpoint, llm_model, and llm_api_key explicitly.

Step 1 — Build the LLM-aware settings.

automl_settings = {
    "algorithm": "<algorithm>",                          # llm / hybrid / autoresearch
    "metric":    "<monitoring-metric>",
    "automl_max_experiments": <max-experiments>,         # typical: 30
    "llm_endpoint": "<llm-endpoint>",                    # e.g. https://inference-api.nvidia.com
    "llm_model":    "<llm-model>",                       # e.g. gcp/google/gemini-3.1-pro-preview
    "llm_api_key":  os.environ.get("AUTOML_LLM_API_KEY") or os.environ["NVIDIA_API_KEY"],
}

Step 2 — Run with those settings. Pass automl_settings to the same runner.run(...) shown in the classical-sweep section above.

Tip: Always timestamp workspace_path. Re-running the same script without a suffix overwrites the previous experiment.

How To Chain AutoML with Data-Efficient Fine-Tuning workflow

Chain hyperparameter search and data-efficient fine-tuning into one workflow: use AutoML to find the best training hyperparameters, then feed the resulting checkpoint into a per-iteration loop that augments the training set and re-trains.

automldata-efficientwarm-startpipeline
Step 0 — Confirm environment variables (before anything else in this flow — even after collecting the action inputs). Collect every action input from the user — never pick one yourself. This covers the model / checkpoint (model_path), gpu_count, num_nodes, the compute backend, and every other <…> placeholder: prompt the user for each before composing the command, and where the guide names a default or recommended value (or you could infer one from the hardware, e.g. reading nvidia-smi to size gpu_count), offer it as a suggestion in that prompt but do not adopt it until the user confirms. This action uses the variables listed below: required ones must already be exported in the shell that launched your agent; optional ones are only needed for the cases noted. Before executing any later step, ask the user to confirm that every required variable below has been set — expect a plain "yes" or "no, let me set them and restart." Ask the user directly; do not probe your own shell to decide this, since the agent's tool shell may not mirror the shell that launched the agent and can report a false "unset" for variables the user has in fact exported. Defer any actual environment check to the moment just before you run the code that consumes these variables. If any required variable is missing, stop here — have the user export it in the launching shell and restart the agent before continuing; do not set it mid-session, since later steps read it from the launching environment.
  • NGC_KEY — NGC API key (container image pulls from nvcr.io; get it at NGC →)
  • ACCESS_KEY + SECRET_KEY — S3-compatible credentials (only if workspace / dataset URIs use s3://)
  • AUTOML_LLM_ENDPOINT + AUTOML_LLM_MODEL + AUTOML_LLM_API_KEY — only if Phase 1 AutoML sweep uses the llm, hybrid, or autoresearch algorithm
  • HF_TOKEN — optional; only if the retrain step uses a gated HuggingFace checkpoint
Also export credentials for your target compute backend — see the matching Platform how-to.

Phase 1 — Run AutoML

Use AutoMLRunner exactly as documented in How To Run AutoML / Hyperparameter Optimization. Sweep N configs over a hyperparameter list, then evaluate the top K configs on a held-out set to guard against mode collapse and pick the best checkpoint.

Step 1 — Imports and SDK.

import os
from datetime import datetime
from tao_sdk.platforms.slurm import SlurmSDK
from tao_sdk.script_runner   import build_entrypoint
from tao_automl.runner       import AutoMLRunner

TIMESTAMP = datetime.now().strftime("%Y%m%d_%H%M%S")
sdk       = SlurmSDK()

Step 2 — Run the AutoML sweep.Use the full settings shown in the AutoML how-to above.

automl_result = AutoMLRunner(sdk=sdk, network_arch="<network>", action="<action>").run(
    train_dataset_uri="<train-dataset-uri>",
    automl_settings={
        "algorithm": "<algorithm>",
        "metric":    "<monitoring-metric>",
        "automl_max_recommendations": <max-recs>,
    },
    workspace_path=f"<workspace-uri>/automl/{TIMESTAMP}",
    gpu_count=<gpu-count>, num_nodes=<num-nodes>, partition="<partition>", account="<account>",
)
BEST_CKPT_URI = automl_result["best"]["checkpoint_uri"]
print("Best AutoML checkpoint:", BEST_CKPT_URI)

Phase 2 — Iterate the Data-Efficient Loop

The data-efficient loop is not a single SDK class — it's a sequence of jobs the agent submits per iteration, seeded with the AutoML best checkpoint. Each iteration runs gap analysis → synthetic data generation → merge → retrain. After each iteration, evaluate to see whether the new dataset moved the metric.

Step 1 — Gap analysis on the current model. Find the weakest slices of the current eval set. Use the data-section how-to for gap analysis on your model family (e.g. VCN AOI Gap Analysis) — that section already shows the exact sdk.create_job(...) call to issue.

Step 2 — Generate synthetic samples for the weak slices.Pick the right data how-to for your modality (e.g. Cosmos AnomalyGen for AOI defects, Cosmos Predict 2.5 for video, or k-NN Mining for real-image augmentation). Submit it via sdk.create_job(...) following that section's example.

Step 3 — Merge the new samples into the training set. This is typically a small data-prep sdk.create_job(...) that runs a merge script (e.g. concatenating Parquet files or updating COCO annotations) and emits a new training dataset URI.

Step 4 — Retrain on the augmented dataset.Submit a new training job seeded with the previous iteration's best checkpoint:

ep_train_iter = build_entrypoint(
    command="<network> train -e /specs/train.yaml",
    inputs={
        "/specs/train.yaml":              "<train-spec-uri-this-iter>",       # spec references the merged data
        "/data/init_checkpoint.pth":      BEST_CKPT_URI,                       # <-- AutoML best (or prior iter's best)
        "/data/train/images.tar.gz":      "<merged-train-images-uri>",
        "/data/train/annotations.json":   "<merged-train-annotations-uri>",
        "/data/eval/images.tar.gz":       "<eval-images-uri>",
        "/data/eval/annotations.json":    "<eval-annotations-uri>",
    },
    outputs=["/results/"],
)
train_iter = sdk.create_job(
    image="<image>",
    command=ep_train_iter["command"],
    gpu_count=<gpu-count>, num_nodes=<num-nodes>,
    env_vars={"HF_TOKEN": os.environ.get("HF_TOKEN", "")},
    partition="<partition>", account="<account>",
)

Step 5 — Wrap steps 1–4 in a Python loop.Thread each iteration's best checkpoint into the next iteration's init_checkpoint, track the per-iteration metric, and stop when it plateaus or you hit your KPI.

current_ckpt = BEST_CKPT_URI
for i in range(<num-iterations>):
    # 1. gap-analysis job  (see data section)
    # 2. synthetic-data job(s)
    # 3. merge job
    # 4. retrain job (above) with /data/init_checkpoint.pth = current_ckpt
    # 5. parse the new checkpoint URI from the retrain job's outputs
    current_ckpt = "<new-best-checkpoint-uri-this-iter>"

How To Serve a TAO Inference Microservice workflow

Serving inference is a regular sdk.create_job(...) call — there is no separate "service" class in the TAO Execution SDK. Pick the right container image and command for the chosen network, submit it as a long-running job, query the HTTP endpoint directly, and call sdk.cancel_job(job.id) to stop.

inferencemicroserviceserving
Step 0 — Confirm environment variables (before anything else in this flow — even after collecting the action inputs). Collect every action input from the user — never pick one yourself. This covers the model / checkpoint (model_path), gpu_count, num_nodes, the compute backend, and every other <…> placeholder: prompt the user for each before composing the command, and where the guide names a default or recommended value (or you could infer one from the hardware, e.g. reading nvidia-smi to size gpu_count), offer it as a suggestion in that prompt but do not adopt it until the user confirms. This action uses the variables listed below: required ones must already be exported in the shell that launched your agent; optional ones are only needed for the cases noted. Before executing any later step, ask the user to confirm that every required variable below has been set — expect a plain "yes" or "no, let me set them and restart." Ask the user directly; do not probe your own shell to decide this, since the agent's tool shell may not mirror the shell that launched the agent and can report a false "unset" for variables the user has in fact exported. Defer any actual environment check to the moment just before you run the code that consumes these variables. If any required variable is missing, stop here — have the user export it in the launching shell and restart the agent before continuing; do not set it mid-session, since later steps read it from the launching environment.
  • NGC_KEY — NGC API key (container image pulls from nvcr.io, required for SlurmSDK)
  • HF_TOKEN — HuggingFace access token with read scope (required if serving a gated model such as nvidia/Cosmos-Reason2-8B or gated Cosmos Predict 2.5 weights)
Also export credentials for your target compute backend — see the matching Platform how-to.
Not every TAO network has an inference-microservice mode. Only three network architectures expose a long-running serving binary. Every other network supports the one-shot <network> inference -e /specs/inference.yaml action documented in its model entry — that runs to completion, writes results, and exits. Reach for the microservice flow below only when the <network-arch> appears in the table.
Network-arch → inference image and command lookup (click to expand)
<network-arch> Container image In-container microservice command
cosmos-rl nvcr.io/nvidia/tao/tao-toolkit:7.0.0-cosmos-rl
(override env var: IMAGE_COSMOS_RL)		 umask 0 && cosmos-rl-inference-microservice --job '<JOB_JSON>' --docker_env_vars '<ENV_JSON>'
Supported models — click to expand

cosmos-rl wants the bare HF repo form (<org>/<repo>), not an hf_model:// URI. The cosmos-rl-inference-microservice binary passes model_path verbatim to huggingface_hub, which rejects scheme-prefixed strings with "Repo id must be in the form 'repo_name' or 'namespace/repo_name'".

model_pathNotes
nvidia/Cosmos-Reason2-8B default Gated — accept agreement at huggingface.co/nvidia/Cosmos-Reason2-8B; set HF_TOKEN
nvidia/Cosmos3-Nano-Reasoner Cosmos Reason 3 (smaller); same gating requirements
cosmos-predict2.5 nvcr.io/nvidia/tao/tao-toolkit:7.0.0-cosmos-predict
(override env var: IMAGE_COSMOS_PREDICT2_5)		 umask 0 && cosmos_predict inference_microservice start <CHECKPOINT_FLAG> --output-dir '/tmp/cosmos-predict2.5-output' --port 8080
Substituting <CHECKPOINT_FLAG> — click to expand

Look at the URI scheme of the user-supplied model_path, then build the flag accordingly:

  • If model_path starts with / (absolute local container path): use --checkpoint-path '<model_path>'. The <model_path> token is the literal value of model_path the user gave you. Example: model_path = "/models/cosmos-predict2.5/ckpt" → --checkpoint-path '/models/cosmos-predict2.5/ckpt'.
  • If model_path starts with hf_model://: use --model '<model-key>'. The <model-key> is not the HF URI — it's a server-side identifier registered in cosmos_predict2.config.MODEL_KEYS that combines the model size, variant, and training stage. Look up the key in the collapsible table below; the table also tells you which key to pick for the user's stated HF repo.
    Click to expand — full MODEL_KEYS registry (derived from cosmos_predict2/config.py)
    --model key HF repository Variant / sub-path Use case (resolution · fps)
    2B/pre-trained nvidia/Cosmos-Predict2.5-2B base / pre-trained 2B base, pre-training only (720p · 16 fps)
    2B/post-trained  default nvidia/Cosmos-Predict2.5-2B base / post-trained 2B base, post-training applied — server default if no --model is passed (720p · 16 fps)
    2B/distilled nvidia/Cosmos-Predict2.5-2B base / distilled 2B base, DMD2 / TrigFlow distilled (fewer denoising steps; 720p · 16 fps)
    14B/pre-trained nvidia/Cosmos-Predict2.5-14B base / pre-trained 14B base, pre-training only (720p · 16 fps)
    14B/post-trained nvidia/Cosmos-Predict2.5-14B base / post-trained 14B base, post-training applied (720p · 16 fps)
    2B/auto/multiview nvidia/Cosmos-Predict2.5-2B auto / multiview Autonomous-driving 7-view multi-camera (720p · 30 fps · 7 views)
    2B/robot/action-cond nvidia/Cosmos-Predict2.5-2B robot / action-cond Robot action-conditioned, Bridge dataset (360p · 4 fps)
    2B/robot/multiview-agibot nvidia/Cosmos-Predict2.5-2B robot / multiview-agibot Robot multi-camera, AgiBot dataset (720p · 16 fps)

    How to map a user-supplied HF URI to the key: parse hf_model://<org>/<repo> into the HF repository column (nvidia/Cosmos-Predict2.5-2B or nvidia/Cosmos-Predict2.5-14B), then pick the row whose Variant / sub-path matches the user's stated training stage or use case. If the user's HF URI matches the 2B or 14B base repo but they don't specify a variant/stage, default to 2B/post-trained (the server's documented default) and confirm with the user before submitting.

    Worked example. User says model_path = "hf_model://nvidia/Cosmos-Predict2.5-2B" and confirms they want the post-trained base model → --model '2B/post-trained'. Set HF_TOKEN in env_vars if the chosen HF repo is gated.

  • If model_path uses a cloud URI scheme (s3://, gs://, az://): reject the input. The inference service has no cloud-storage dependency and these URIs are not accepted — ask the user for a local container path or an hf_model:// URI instead.

Example (post-trained 2B base model from HuggingFace):

umask 0 && cosmos_predict inference_microservice start --model '2B/post-trained' --output-dir '/tmp/cosmos-predict2.5-output' --port 8080
tao-dataservices
(HuggingFace inference servers)		 nvcr.io/nvidia/tao/tao-toolkit:7.0.0-data-services
(override env var: IMAGE_TAO_DATASERVICES)		 umask 0 && python -m nvidia_tao_core.microservices.handlers.huggingface_inference_microservice_server --port 8080 --job '<JOB_JSON>' --docker_env_vars '<ENV_JSON>'
Supported models — click to expand

Generic HuggingFace loader — auto-detects LLM, VLM, diffusion, or image-classification from the model. Pass any hf_model://<org>/<model> URI or a local container path. Set HF_TOKEN for gated repos.

model_path (example)Type
hf_model://Qwen/Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct default example VLM — used by the Qwen VLM captioning pipeline
hf_model://Qwen/Qwen3-VL-235B-A22B-Instruct VLM — larger Qwen3 variant
hf_model://nvidia/Cosmos-Reason1-7B VLM — earlier Cosmos Reason generation
any HuggingFace model LLM, VLM, diffusion, or image-classification — loader auto-detects

<JOB_JSON> and <ENV_JSON> are json.dumps(...) strings of the job payload and env payload, respectively, then shlex.quote(...)'d so they survive shell parsing. The job payload includes job_id, neural_network_name, and specs.model_path — its accepted form is network-specific: cosmos-rl takes a local container path or a bare HF repo (<org>/<repo>, no scheme prefix); cosmos-predict2.5 takes a local container path or an hf_model://<org>/<repo> URI (resolved to a --model key via the registry); tao-dataservices takes a local container path or an hf_model://<org>/<model> URI. Cloud URIs such as s3:// are not accepted by any network.

Walked example with placeholders. Each step below is independently copyable. Before you start, pick the row in the lookup table above that matches the network you want to serve, and note three values from that row:

  • <network-arch> — the network identifier (cosmos-rl, cosmos-predict2.5, or tao-dataservices).
  • <inference-image> — the container image from the "Container image" column.
  • <microservice-command> — the command template from the "In-container microservice command" column, kept verbatim (its <JOB_JSON> / <ENV_JSON> tokens are filled in below).

The walked example follows the cosmos-rl / tao-dataservices shape (both consume a JOB_JSON + ENV_JSON payload). For cosmos-predict2.5 the command template uses --checkpoint-path or --model flags directly and does not consume JSON payloads — skip Steps 2–4 below and use Variant B in Step 5 to substitute <CHECKPOINT_FLAG> per the rules in the table above.

Supported backends and how each reaches port 8080. create_job(...) takes no ports= kwarg on any backend. Each backend reaches the container's port 8080 differently:
  • DockerSDK — set DOCKER_NETWORK=host in the environment before instantiating DockerSDK(). The handler launches the container with network=host, sharing the host's network namespace — port 8080 inside the container is port 8080 on the host. Step 7's BASE_URL is http://localhost:8080.
  • SlurmSDK — Step 7 opens an SSH port-forward from your agent host to the allocated compute node.
  • KubernetesSDK — expose the job pod with a Service of type LoadBalancer on port 8080 after submitting (typically via kubectl expose).
  • BrevSDK — same trick as DockerSDK at the OS level (Brev runs containers on single instances). Set DOCKER_NETWORK=host if your Brev instance image honors it; otherwise the BrevSDK has no first-class port-publishing path. Treat Brev as best-effort for this flow.
Prerequisites. The general training prerequisites for your chosen backend apply unchanged — env vars exported per How To Export All Required Environment Variables, plus the SDK install and platform-specific setup documented in the matching Platform how-to (DockerSDK, BrevSDK, SlurmSDK, KubernetesSDK). Two extras specific to running a microservice:
  • NGC image-pull credentials must be available to the compute backend. Kubernetes needs a docker-registry Secret (pass via image_pull_secret="<secret-name>"); DockerSDK and SlurmSDK pick up $NGC_KEY from env_vars at submit time; Brev needs docker login nvcr.io on the target instance once.
  • SlurmSDK only: Step 7 opens an SSH port-forward from your agent host to the allocated cluster node. The passwordless-SSH session you set up in How To Run TAO on a SLURM Cluster is reused — no extra credentials needed, but the session must exist.

Starting the Inference Service

Step 1 — Imports and a unique job id.Open a Python session, import the SDK module for your chosen backend, and pick any unique string for JOB_ID — it will be reused in two places below.

import os, json, shlex, requests
from tao_sdk.platforms.docker import DockerSDK   # or SlurmSDK, KubernetesSDK, BrevSDK

JOB_ID = "<job-id>"   # any unique string

Step 2 — Build the job payload.Set neural_network_name to the <network-arch> you picked from the lookup table. The inference service has no cloud-storage dependency. The accepted form of model_path depends on the network — see the prose above the walked example for the per-network rules. Cloud URIs (s3://, gs://, az://) are rejected by all networks.

job_payload = {
    "job_id":               JOB_ID,
    "neural_network_name":  "<network-arch>",        # from the lookup table above
    "specs": {"model_path": "<hf-model-or-local-path>"},
}

Step 3 — Build the env payload. Set TAO_EXECUTION_BACKEND to the string matching the SDK class you instantiate in Step 5.

env_payload = {
    "TAO_EXECUTION_BACKEND": "<execution-backend>",   # "docker" for DockerSDK,
                                                       # "brev" for BrevSDK,
                                                       # "slurm" for SlurmSDK,
                                                       # "kubernetes" for KubernetesSDK
    "TAO_API_JOB_ID":        JOB_ID,                   # must equal job_payload["job_id"]
    "CLOUD_BASED":           "False",                  # literal string; inference has no cloud dependency
}

Step 4 — Shell-quote the JSON payloads.Quote them with shlex.quote(json.dumps(...)) so they survive parsing inside the container command.

JOB_JSON = shlex.quote(json.dumps(job_payload, separators=(",", ":")))
ENV_JSON = shlex.quote(json.dumps(env_payload, separators=(",", ":")))

Step 5 — Build the in-container command.Paste the <microservice-command> template verbatim from the lookup table for your <network-arch>, then substitute its literal angle-bracket tokens. The substitution differs per network — pick the variant below that matches the network you chose.

Variant A — cosmos-rl / tao-dataservices. The template contains '<JOB_JSON>' and '<ENV_JSON>' tokens; replace them with the shell-quoted strings from Step 4.

COMMAND_TEMPLATE = "<microservice-command>"   # paste verbatim from the lookup table
command = (
    COMMAND_TEMPLATE
    .replace("'<JOB_JSON>'", JOB_JSON)
    .replace("'<ENV_JSON>'", ENV_JSON)
)

Variant B — cosmos-predict2.5. The template contains only <CHECKPOINT_FLAG> (no JSON tokens — cosmos-predict2.5 does not accept --job / --docker_env_vars, which is why Steps 2–4 are skipped). Build checkpoint_flag per the substitution rules in the lookup table — local path → --checkpoint-path '<path>'; hf_model:// → --model '<model-key>' (look the key up in the collapsed MODEL_KEYS registry); cloud URIs are rejected.

import shlex

# Pick ONE branch based on the user-supplied model_path:
model_path = "<model_path>"   # e.g. "/models/cosmos-predict2.5/ckpt" or "hf_model://nvidia/Cosmos-Predict2.5-2B"

if model_path.startswith("/"):
    checkpoint_flag = f"--checkpoint-path {shlex.quote(model_path)}"
elif model_path.startswith("hf_model://"):
    registered_model_key = "<model-key>"   # from the MODEL_KEYS registry (e.g. "2B/post-trained")
    checkpoint_flag = f"--model {shlex.quote(registered_model_key)}"
else:
    raise ValueError(f"Unsupported model_path scheme: {model_path!r}")

COMMAND_TEMPLATE = "<microservice-command>"   # paste verbatim from the lookup table
command = COMMAND_TEMPLATE.replace("<CHECKPOINT_FLAG>", checkpoint_flag)

Step 6 — Submit the long-running job.Use the <inference-image> from the lookup table for your chosen <network-arch>. The shape of platform-specific kwargs differs per backend — expand the block below to see the right kwargs for the SDK you instantiated in Step 1.

job = sdk.create_job(
    image="<inference-image>",   # from the lookup table for your <network-arch>
    command=command,
    gpu_count=<gpu-count>,
    env_vars={"HF_TOKEN": os.environ.get("HF_TOKEN", "")},   # required for gated HF models
    # ...plus the platform-specific kwargs from the table below
)

Platform-specific kwargs: for the regular kwargs each SDK expects (mounts / shm_size on Docker, instance_id / gpu_type on Brev, partition / account on SLURM, namespace / node_selector / tolerations on Kubernetes), follow the platform how-to that matches the SDK you imported in Step 1 — the same kwargs that section uses for training apply here. Exposing the service port is backend-specific:

  • DockerSDK — set DOCKER_NETWORK=host in the environment before DockerSDK() is instantiated; the handler launches the container in host network mode and port 8080 is reachable as localhost:8080 on the host.
  • BrevSDK — same trick at the OS level (containers run on a single instance). Set DOCKER_NETWORK=host if the Brev instance honors it; otherwise BrevSDK has no first-class port-publishing path.
  • SlurmSDK — no port mapping at submit time; Step 7 opens an SSH port-forward instead.
  • KubernetesSDK — expose the job pod with a Service of type LoadBalancer targeting port 8080 (typically via kubectl expose after the job is submitted).

Step 7 — Discover the base URL.How you find the URL depends on which compute backend you submitted the job to — it is not printed verbatim in the job logs for every backend. Expand the section below for the per-backend procedure, then assign the result to BASE_URL.

Per-backend base-URL discovery — click to expand
Backend How to obtain <base-url>
DockerSDK
(local Docker)		 Submit with DOCKER_NETWORK=host exported in the environment that creates the SDK; the container shares the host's network namespace. <base-url> is http://localhost:8080 on the SDK host.
BrevSDK If DOCKER_NETWORK=host works on your Brev instance, the service binds the instance's public IP at port 8080 — <base-url> is http://<brev-instance-ip>:8080. Find the IP via brev ls or the Brev console. Best-effort: BrevSDK has no first-class port-publishing path.
SlurmSDK The container runs on an allocated compute node, not on a publicly routable host. Find the node name via squeue --job <slurm-job-id>, then open an SSH port-forward from the agent host to the node: ssh -L <host-port>:<node>:8080 <login-host>. After that, <base-url> is http://localhost:<host-port>.
KubernetesSDK Expose the job pod with a Service of type LoadBalancer on port 8080, wait for the external IP to be allocated (kubectl get svc <svc-name> -w), then use http://<external-ip>:8080 as <base-url>.

Step 8 — Wait for readiness.Poll GET <base-url>/api/v1/health/readiness until the service signals it's ready. Three signals to handle:

  • Connection refused — the container hasn't bound port 8080 yet. Catch requests.exceptions.ConnectionError, sleep, and retry.
  • HTTP 503 with body {"status":"not_ready","reason":"model_loading", …} — the service is up but the model is still loading. This is not a failure — keep polling.
  • HTTP 200 with body {"status":"ready", …} — service is ready to accept inference requests. Break out of the loop.

Any other shape — non-200 with a body whose reason is not model_loading (for example a model-load failure that leaves the Flask server up but stuck) — is a real failure and should raise. Check the response body, not the HTTP code alone: the service reuses 503 for both transient-loading and failure states, differentiated only by the reason field.

import time, requests

BASE_URL = "<base-url>"            # from Step 7 above
while True:
    try:
        r = requests.get(f"{BASE_URL}/api/v1/health/readiness", timeout=5)
    except requests.exceptions.ConnectionError:
        time.sleep(5)             # container hasn't bound 8080 yet
        continue
    try:
        body = r.json()
    except ValueError:
        body = {}
    if r.status_code == 200 and body.get("status") == "ready":
        break                     # ready to serve requests
    if r.status_code == 503 and body.get("reason") == "model_loading":
        time.sleep(5)             # still loading the model
        continue
    raise RuntimeError(f"Service failed to start: HTTP {r.status_code} {r.text}")

Sending an Inference Request

All three networks expose the OpenAI-compatible POST <base-url>/v1/chat/completions endpoint. The body uses the standard messages list with role+content items; per-network differences are summarised in the collapsible block below.

Step 1 — Build the request body.The body always contains a messages list. Each item has a role ("system" or "user") and a content list whose entries are {type: "text", text: …}, {type: "image_url", image_url: {url: …}}, or {type: "video_url", video_url: {url: …}}. Media URLs must be either inline data:<mime>;base64,… URIs or plain http(s):// URLs — cloud URIs (s3://, gs://, az://) are rejected.

Where the media fetch actually happens. For http(s):// URLs, the container performs the GET — not your client. Any host that blocks no-User-Agent or automated traffic (Wikimedia is the common offender) returns 403 Forbidden to the container, surfacing as HTTP 500 on /v1/chat/completions with a body like {"error":{"message":"403 Client Error: Forbidden for url: …","type":"internal_error"}}. If you hit that and the model is otherwise loaded, switch the offending URL to an inline data:<mime>;base64,… URI — it removes the network dependency entirely and is the most reliable form for testing.
body = {
    "messages": [
        {
            "role":    "user",
            "content": [
                {"type": "text",  "text":  "<your-prompt>"},
                # optional media items — required for some networks (see below):
                # {"type": "image_url", "image_url": {"url": "data:image/png;base64,<base64-bytes>"}},
                # {"type": "video_url", "video_url": {"url": "https://example.com/clip.mp4"}},
            ],
        },
    ],
    # Sampling parameters — see the per-network notes below for what each
    # network actually consumes. Omit fields the network doesn't support.
    "max_tokens":  <max-tokens>,
    "top_p":       <top-p>,
    "temperature": <temperature>,
}
Per-network request-body constraints — click to expand
<network-arch> Media required? Sampling fields consumed Response shape
cosmos-rl Yes — every request MUST include at least one image_url or video_url item (cosmos-rl is a VLM). Images: .jpg .jpeg .png .bmp .tiff. Videos: .mp4 .mkv .webm .avi .mov. max_tokens (default 1024), top_p (default 0.8), temperature (default 0.7, range 0.0–2.0). Plain text in choices[0].message.content.
cosmos-predict2.5 Optional — the content of the first user message decides the inference type: text only → TEXT2WORLD, image item → IMAGE2WORLD, video item → VIDEO2WORLD. Images: .png .jpg .jpeg .webp. Videos: .mp4. max_tokens is repurposed as num_output_frames (default 77). temperature and top_p are not consumed — omit them. Diffusion knobs: guidance (0–7, default 7), num_steps (default 35), seed (default 0). Generated video as a base64 data:video/mp4;base64,… URI inside choices[0].message.content.
tao-dataservices
(HuggingFace inference)		 Depends on the loaded HF model. The endpoint accepts text-only requests; the underlying handler may reject them for VLM/diffusion models. max_tokens, top_p, temperature — pass-through to the loaded HF model's expectations. Pass-through (typically plain text in choices[0].message.content).

Step 2 — POST the request.Submit to the /v1/chat/completions endpoint on the base URL from Step 7. A 300-second client timeout is recommended (cosmos-predict2.5 video generation can take minutes).

resp = requests.post(
    f"{BASE_URL}/v1/chat/completions",
    json=body,
    headers={"Content-Type": "application/json"},
    timeout=300,
)
resp.raise_for_status()
print(resp.json()["choices"][0]["message"]["content"])

Stopping the Inference Service

Step 1 — Cancel the job.The container shuts down and the GPU is released.

sdk.cancel_job(job.id)
Idle-timeout auto-deletion. Independent of cancel_job, the inference microservice runs an internal health monitor that requests auto-deletion after 30 minutes of idle time — visible in container logs as Server has been idle for 30.0 minutes (timeout: 30). Requesting auto-deletion.. The actual deletion is performed via a status callback to a TAO workflow service and is only fired when both TAO_LOGGING_SERVER_URL and TAO_ADMIN_KEY are exported in the container's environment. With those unset (the default for ad-hoc submissions), the auto-deletion request is logged but skipped — the container keeps running until you call sdk.cancel_job(job.id). To keep a service alive past 30 minutes of idleness on a deployment that does set those env vars, send a heartbeat request (e.g. a cheap GET /api/v1/health/readiness) inside the window, or unset TAO_LOGGING_SERVER_URL / TAO_ADMIN_KEY for that job's env_vars dict.

How To Fine-Tune a HuggingFace Model with TAO workflow

End-to-end fine-tune, evaluation, inference, and HF Hub push for any HuggingFace CV or VLM model. The TAO Execution SDK submits the job to your chosen managed backend; the agent inside the container fetches the model card, the author's finetune example, and HF task docs before generating code.

finetuninghuggingfacecomputer-visiontraining
Step 0 — Confirm environment variables (before anything else in this flow — even after collecting the action inputs). Collect every action input from the user — never pick one yourself. This covers the model / checkpoint (model_path), gpu_count, num_nodes, the compute backend, and every other <…> placeholder: prompt the user for each before composing the command, and where the guide names a default or recommended value (or you could infer one from the hardware, e.g. reading nvidia-smi to size gpu_count), offer it as a suggestion in that prompt but do not adopt it until the user confirms. This action uses the variables listed below: required ones must already be exported in the shell that launched your agent; optional ones are only needed for the cases noted. Before executing any later step, ask the user to confirm that every required variable below has been set — expect a plain "yes" or "no, let me set them and restart." Ask the user directly; do not probe your own shell to decide this, since the agent's tool shell may not mirror the shell that launched the agent and can report a false "unset" for variables the user has in fact exported. Defer any actual environment check to the moment just before you run the code that consumes these variables. If any required variable is missing, stop here — have the user export it in the launching shell and restart the agent before continuing; do not set it mid-session, since later steps read it from the launching environment.
  • NGC_KEY — NGC API key (container image pulls from nvcr.io; get it at NGC →)
  • HF_TOKEN — HuggingFace access token (read scope for model/dataset download; write scope if using --push-to-hub)
  • WANDB_API_KEY — Weights & Biases API key (required for run tracking)
  • WANDB_PROJECT — Weights & Biases project name (consumed at runtime by os.environ["WANDB_PROJECT"] inside the container — job crashes with a KeyError if missing)
Also export credentials for your target compute backend — see the matching Platform how-to.

Supported tasks: image-classification, object-detection, semantic-segmentation, instance-segmentation, depth-estimation, image-text-to-text (VLM SFT / LoRA), LLM SFT / DPO / GRPO.

Required env vars: HF_TOKEN (read scope for inputs, write scope for Hub push), WANDB_API_KEY, WANDB_PROJECT.

import os
from tao_sdk.platforms.slurm import SlurmSDK
from tao_sdk.script_runner   import build_entrypoint

sdk = SlurmSDK()
ep  = build_entrypoint(
    command=(
        "tao-hf-finetune run"
        " --model <hf-model-id>"              # e.g. microsoft/swin-tiny-patch4-window7-224
        " --task <hf-task>"                   # e.g. image-classification
        " --dataset <hf-dataset-id>"          # e.g. food101
        " --push-to-hub <hf-user>/<hf-repo>"
        " --output /results"
    ),
    outputs=["/results/"],
)
job = sdk.create_job(
    image="nvcr.io/nvidia/tao/tao-toolkit:7.0.0-pyt",
    command=ep["command"],
    gpu_count=<gpu-count>, num_nodes=<num-nodes>,
    env_vars={
        "HF_TOKEN":      os.environ["HF_TOKEN"],
        "WANDB_API_KEY": os.environ["WANDB_API_KEY"],
        "WANDB_PROJECT": os.environ["WANDB_PROJECT"],
    },
)

How To Run the Cosmos-RL Video-QA Improvement Loop workflow

Iterative data-augmentation workflow for Cosmos-RL video QA. There is no single SDK class — the loop is a sequence of jobs the agent submits per iteration: gap analysis → Qwen captioning → Cosmos Predict 2.5 video generation → merge → fine-tune. Each iteration produces a better checkpoint; you stop when the validation metric plateaus or you hit your KPI.

videoqacosmosdata-efficientsynthetic-dataiterative
Step 0 — Confirm environment variables (before anything else in this flow — even after collecting the action inputs). Collect every action input from the user — never pick one yourself. This covers the model / checkpoint (model_path), gpu_count, num_nodes, the compute backend, and every other <…> placeholder: prompt the user for each before composing the command, and where the guide names a default or recommended value (or you could infer one from the hardware, e.g. reading nvidia-smi to size gpu_count), offer it as a suggestion in that prompt but do not adopt it until the user confirms. Before executing any later step, ask the user to confirm that every required variable below has been set — expect a plain "yes" or "no, let me set them and restart." Ask the user directly; do not probe your own shell to decide this, since the agent's tool shell may not mirror the shell that launched the agent and can report a false "unset" for variables the user has in fact exported. Defer any actual environment check to the moment just before you run the code that consumes these variables.
